### Nextment Challenges in the country to engage effective and sustainable implementation of REDD+ activities.

The Government of the Republic of the Union of Myanmar (GoR) is committed to implement the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) and the Enhanced Transparency (ET) through the support of UNDP. To this end, the GoR has developed a National Strategy for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+), which includes the development of a Green Climate Fund (GCF) Readiness Preparation Proposal (RPP). The RBP aims to address the challenges faced by the country in implementing REDD+ activities, including the need for capacity building, institutional strengthening, and the development of a robust monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) system.

Consistent with the GCF and FAO policies, the proposal is designed to enhance the institutional capacities at national, regional, and local levels. The proposal aims to strengthen the institutional capacities in English and the local languages, all relevant staff will be trained on the use of the new MRV system. This will ensure that the data collected is accurate and culturally appropriate. Such disclosure of relevant project information will also enhance transparency and accountability.

The support includes the development of a comprehensive MRV system that meets the needs of the country, including the development of a national MRV strategy and action plan. This will ensure that the data collected is accurate and culturally appropriate.

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Common Committee Development Management Meeting #141

### Additional relevant regulatory framework (a) PRL:

- Environmental Impact Assessments (EIAs) are integral to the environmental licensing process and are required for projects that may cause significant environmental impact. The EIA process includes the identification of potential impacts, the development of mitigation measures, and monitoring of the project and the level of its compliance with regulations. EIAs are subject to the Environmental Act.

- In the context of the management of customary lands, PRL is a regime of recognized Land Owners and Businesses that have a right to manage their land and business in accordance with the Land Ownership Act 2014. The Customary Land Ownership Act 2014, the Climate Change Agreement Act 2015, and other legislation and regulations are relevant.

- Regarding customary payments' rights to the land, PRL is of the view that it is a matter of customary ownership and management. PRL is subject to the Customary Land Ownership Act 2014. The Customary Land Ownership Act 2014 provides for the recognition of customary rights and the management of customary lands.

### Governance and business establishment:

During project design and implementation, individuals or groups may have claims over areas affected by the project. These claims should be addressed through a process that is consistent with PRL. PRL will ensure that these claims are properly documented and resolved in accordance with the law.

Over the medium term, PRL (CROA) aims to join GRI procedures to track stakeholders' concerns more effectively. This will include the development of a grievance redress mechanism and the establishment of a complaints process. The aim is to ensure that all stakeholders have access to an effective grievance redress mechanism.

At the national level, several institutions handled complaints, including the PRPA, which handled disputes between landowners and businesses. The PRPA also handled disputes between individuals and communities. The PRPA was established under the Customary Land Ownership Act 2014. The PRPA is responsible for resolving disputes related to customary lands.

Sub-national mechanisms include additional units and bodies that collect and resolve complaints at the local level, such as the Village Courts, which operate under the Customary Land Ownership Act 2014. These courts are responsible for resolving disputes between individuals and communities over land ownership and management. The Village Courts operate under the Customary Land Ownership Act 2014.

At the national level, the PRPA is responsible for handling complaints related to customary lands. The PRPA is established under the Customary Land Ownership Act 2014. The PRPA is responsible for resolving disputes between individuals and communities over land ownership and management.

Green Climate Fund Project ID: x

The REDD+ National Adaptation and Capacity Building Strategy (2015-2030) aims to strengthen and comprehensive framework for climate change adaptation and mitigation in the country. The REDD+ Readiness Preparation Proposal (R-PP) was a comprehensive readiness process that consolidated systems, policies, and procedures to ensure effective implementation of REDD+. This proposal aims to generate income, promote good ethics, and achieve sustainable development of REDD+.

## During 2014-2015: RBP Implementation Key Results

- Addressed vulnerability and expansion of livelihoods.
- Improved governance with the inclusion of local communities and stakeholders in decision-making processes.
- Enhanced capacity to manage climate change risks and opportunities.
- Strengthened institutional arrangements for sustainable development.

## Legal and Policy Frameworks Supporting Implementation

- The REDD+ RBP is aligned with the country's climate change policies, including the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), and the Paris Agreement.
- The REDD+ Strategy Development during the RBP aligns with the country's NDCs and the Paris Agreement.

### National REDD+ Strategy Development

The development of the national REDD+ strategy was a participatory process involving various stakeholders, including:

- Government agencies
- Civil society organizations
- Indigenous peoples' organizations
- Private sector entities

## Investments Made in Technology Transfer

The RBP has made significant investments to enhance technology transfer and capacity building. Key achievements include:

- Development of guidelines for climate-smart agriculture.
- Support for the development of climate-resilient infrastructure.
- Establishment of a national climate change information system.

### Technology Transfer and Capacity Building

The RBP has supported the development of a comprehensive framework for technology transfer, including:

- Training programs on climate-smart agriculture.
- Workshops on climate resilience strategies.
- Development of guidelines for climate adaptation planning.

## Enterprise Investment and Management

The RBP has facilitated enterprise investment and management by providing support to small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs). Key achievements include:

- Establishment of a national SME development fund.
- Provision of technical assistance to SMEs.
- Development of a national SME policy framework.

### Enterprise Investment and Management

The RBP has supported the establishment of a comprehensive framework for enterprise investment and management, including:

- Development of a national SME development fund.
- Provision of technical assistance to SMEs.
- Development of a national SME policy framework.

## Stakeholder Engagement and Capacity Building

The RBP has engaged with various stakeholders to promote awareness, knowledge sharing, and capacity building. Key achievements include:

- Conducted training programs on climate change adaptation and mitigation.
- Facilitated the development of community-based adaptation plans.
- Provided technical assistance to local governments.

### Stakeholder Engagement and Capacity Building

The RBP has engaged with various stakeholders to promote awareness, knowledge sharing, and capacity building. Key achievements include:

- Conducted training programs on climate change adaptation and mitigation.
- Facilitated the development of community-based adaptation plans.
- Provided technical assistance to local governments.

### Selection, analysis, and scoring

These works discuss on improving the quality and reliability of data collection, framework implementation during 2014-2016 and selected PNG's existing functions systems for monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) activities. ### 2.2 For the use of funds

#### Additional risks and regulatory support

The proposed project will be implemented through national programs that have strong political backing and direct involvement from the PNG Government.

The **REDD+** project is in line with the government's commitment to implement a global policy - REDD+, which is voluntary attaching to Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and promoting sustainable development. The project will be implemented through the Department of Environment and Conservation (DEC), Climate Change and Development Authority (CCDA), and Climate Change and Development Foundation (CCDF).

The project will be implemented in line with the following policies:

- **National Climate Change Policy 2015**: Provides a framework for coordinated national planning.
- **National Climate Change Strategy 2014**: Establishes systems for NDCs, MRV, and adaptation to climate change.
- **Climate Change Act 2013**: Provides a legal framework for the establishment of an independent Climate Change Commission (CCC).
- **Environmental Management Act 2013**: Provides a legal framework for environmental management.

The project also considers with the legal and regulatory framework, minimizing risks of conflicts, legal disputes, or other issues that may arise from the implementation of the project. The project will be implemented by government agencies, community leaders, and communities and will be supported by the private sector.

The project will be implemented by a mechanism to ensure transparent governance and accountability towards to the national climate change policy and strategy. The project will also be implemented in line with the principles of transparency, accountability, participation, and sustainability.

**FAQ**: As per FAQ at the GCF website, it is important to note that the **REDD+** project management needs to simply the process and ensure that the project is implemented in a timely manner. The project will also be implemented in line with the principles of transparency, accountability, participation, and sustainability.

FAO adopted the Framework for Environment and Social Management (FESM). The FESM establishes a set of principles and standards to guide FAO in managing environmental and social risks. It includes key elements such as environmental and social impact assessments, which are essential when planning activities that affect the environment.

### FESM Framework

The FESM framework is underpinned by new ESS, which reflects the organization’s commitment to mainstream environmental and social considerations into all FAO activities. The change of the indicator data management system is closely linked with this. The FESM framework also includes a set of tools that support the implementation of the Framework. These include:

- Environmental and Social Impact Assessments (ESIA) which are used to identify potential impacts on the environment.
- Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMP) which outline how environmental and social risks will be managed.

### Functions

FAO has a set of policies that support the implementation of environmental and social safeguards. These include:

- FAO Environmental and Social Safeguard Policies: These policies provide guidance on how to manage environmental and social impacts from any development activities of FAO projects and other activities.
- FAO Environmental and Social Safeguard Procedures: These procedures provide a framework for implementing the safeguard policies.

As part of GCF (at FAO) projects follow the due diligence having to ensure that any environmental and social safeguards are met. When funds are allocated, they are used for conservation purposes for risk and impacts the environmentally sound management of natural resources.

### Funding Proposal

FAO is requesting $10 million USD from GCF to support REDD+ activities in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC).

#### E.1 Helping to ensure that the environmental benefits are not excluded in the integration activities.

Comparative Advantages of FAO

With well-established presence through the FAO Representation in Kinshasa, the organization supports various stakeholders and communities in the DRC. The organization has a strong network with local governments and communities, which is essential for implementing REDD+ activities.

The FAO Country Programming Framework (CPF) for DRC focuses on sustainable resource management, food security, and nutrition through climate-smart agriculture and forestry. This aligns well with the objectives of the GCF funding, which strengthen agricultural and forestry value chains.

FAO has a strong track record in implementing large-scale projects related to natural resources management. FAO is well-positioned to implement REDD+ activities due to its extensive experience in managing natural resource programs.

As an implementing agency of the UN-REDD Programme, FAO has gained valuable experience in supporting REDD+ implementation through various phases including project preparation, implementation, and monitoring.

Prior to the Mission to the Climate Change in Agriculture (IMCCA) and the Approving Strategy in Climate Change (ICCA), FAO's activities in implementing Climate Change projects have been recognized globally.

FAO commitment is deeply rooted and institutional strengthening is achieved by advancing the capacity of local stakeholders. The organization has a strong record of working with local communities, which enhances the project's sustainability. FAO actively engages with local governments to ensure that the project meets their needs and aligns with national priorities.

Key activities include:

- Supporting the development of REDD+ strategies and policies.
- Enhancing capacity building for local stakeholders in climate-smart agriculture and forestry.
- Promoting sustainable land management practices.
- Strengthening community-based natural resource management systems.
- Facilitating access to finance for smallholder farmers and communities.

In addition, FAO has extensive experience in the implementation of projects that address environmental safeguards. The organization has a strong track record in implementing projects that are environmentally sound and socially inclusive. FAO's work in the DRC focuses on improving livelihoods through sustainable resource management.

- Vulnerability Assessments on the Representative Governance of Forests, Lands, and Resources.
- Support to Adaptation and Mitigation Strategies for Climate Change.
- FAO's Land Tenure and Administration Technical Guidelines.

FAO is committed to ensuring that the project contributes to achieving the objectives of the GCF and supports adaptation and mitigation efforts in the DRC.

- Enhancing Governance of Tenure: Enhances institutional coordination and collaboration.

### Solutions for Open Land Administration (SOLA)
- The SOLA suite provides technical solutions for the management and administration of land rights, including:
  - Land registration
  - Land valuation
  - Land monitoring and enforcement
  - Land administration
  - Land tenure rights at all tenurial levels. It can be applied by PMU's offices and other government agencies to support land administration.

### Tenure Conflict Resolution (TCR)
- TCR is a conflict resolution tool that provides:
  - A comprehensive approach to tenure conflicts.
  - Aims to resolve conflicts through mediation, negotiation, and conciliation.

### Sustainable Forest Management (SFM) Module
- SFM is a comprehensive digital platform that offers:
  - Tools for forest monitoring and management.
  - Supports REDD+ activities and sustainable forest management.

### Open Forests Data for Forest Monitoring and Resource Assessment
- Includes several tools to support forest monitoring, resource assessment, and group data collection.
  - Tools include: Open Forests, Open Data Cube (ODC), and GeoNode.

- The FFP is a global program that supports smallholders and indigenous peoples' organizations in sustainable forest management and agroforestry.

### Recommendations for Improving Gender Inclusiveness and Participation in Phase III Projects

### Context of the Gender Inclusiveness Proposal

The proposal aims to enhance gender inclusiveness and participation in REDD+ projects. It builds upon existing programs and provides additional support for gender equality and women's empowerment.

#### Main Objectives

1. **Mainstream Gender**: Integrate gender into all project activities.
2. **Capacity Building**: Provide training on gender mainstreaming.
3. **Gender Action Plan**: Develop a comprehensive plan to address gender issues.

### Implementation Strategy

The strategy includes:

- **Training and Workshops**: Conduct workshops for local communities, focusing on gender equality and women's empowerment.
- **Partnerships**: Collaborate with local organizations to enhance capacity building efforts.
- **Monitoring and Evaluation**: Implement monitoring systems to track progress towards gender goals.

### Gender Action Plan

The plan includes:

1. **Training Programs**: Develop training programs for local communities, focusing on gender equality and women's empowerment.
2. **Workshops**: Conduct workshops with local organizations to enhance capacity building efforts.
3. **Monitoring Systems**: Implement monitoring systems to track progress towards gender goals.

### Project Budget

The project budget is outlined as follows:

### Project Timeline

- **Year 1**: Focus on training programs and initial workshops.
- **Year 2**: Conduct additional workshops and implement monitoring systems.

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (Page 8 of 9)

### Strengthening Public Confidence: By effectively addressing corruption, it is a strategy to increase and strengthen public confidence in government institutions. This promotes the welfare of people and ensures that the benefits of natural resources are fairly distributed. It also enhances community participation and support for broader socio-economic development.

## Key Objectives:

- **Prevention:** This includes implementing preventive measures to reduce the likelihood of corrupt activities, such as:
  - Establishing clear guidelines and procedures.
  - Ensuring transparency in all operations.
  - Encouraging a culture of integrity.
- **Enforcement:** This involves enforcing laws and regulations to ensure compliance with anti-corruption policies. It includes:
  - Strengthening law enforcement agencies.
  - Enhancing the independence of investigative bodies.
  - Promoting public participation in monitoring corruption.

## Institutional Reforms:

The strategy calls for the establishment and reinforcement of key institutions, the strengthening of their capacities, and the promotion of good governance practices to ensure effective implementation. This includes:
- **Strengthening Judicial Systems:** Ensuring that the judicial system is independent and impartial.
- **Promoting Transparency:** Encouraging openness in government operations to enhance accountability.
- **Improving Public Services:** Enhancing the quality of public services to reduce opportunities for corruption.

## Implementation Mechanisms:

The successful implementation of these strategies requires:
- **Capacity Building:** Providing training and resources to build capacity within institutions.
  - **Training Programs:** Developing and delivering training programs for government officials.
  - **Technical Assistance:** Offering technical assistance to support reform efforts.
- **Public Awareness Campaigns:** Raising awareness about corruption and its impact on society.

## Challenges Addressed:

The strategy acknowledges the significant challenges of institutional development, which include:
- **Resource Constraints:** Limited financial resources can hinder progress in anti-corruption efforts.
  - **Limited Public Awareness:** A lack of public understanding of corruption issues can limit support for reform initiatives.
  - **Political Will:** The commitment and support from political leaders are essential for successful implementation.

## Success Indicators:

The success of the strategy will be measured by:
- **Reduced Corruption Levels:** A decrease in reported cases of corruption.
- **Increased Public Confidence:** Higher levels of public trust in government institutions.
- **Enhanced Institutional Capacity:** Improved capabilities and effectiveness of anti-corruption agencies.

1. **Fund Risk Management**
   - Fund risks must be identified and mitigated across all areas, with a focus on financial risk management.
   - FAO will ensure that the fund is managed in accordance with the policies and procedures of the FAO Management, Regional, and Country Offices to support the establishment and monitoring of risk management frameworks.

2. **Training and Responsibilities**
   - FAO provides mandatory training for all its personnel on risk management.
   - Training will be conducted annually by the Office of the Controller and Budget (OCB) and will include an overview of risk management principles, practices, and tools.
   - Specific Units at Headquarters: Heads of units are responsible for developing local risks and implementing risk management frameworks.

3. **Risks and Responsibilities**
   - FAO is committed to the highest standards of integrity and efficiency and is obligated to support any response to fraud, corruption, or misuse of resources.
   - The Office of the Controller and Budget (OCB) is responsible for the development and implementation of internal control systems.
   - OCB will ensure that all units are aware of their responsibilities in maintaining effective controls.

4. **Internal Controls**
   - FAO must have an internal control system with its various units, including educational and technical support staff, organization, structure of offices, and other branches.
   - Internal controls must be designed to provide reasonable assurance regarding the reliability of financial reporting and compliance with applicable laws and regulations.

5. **Reporting Fraud**
   - FAO personnel are obligated to report any personal fraud or corrupt practices.
   - Reports of suspected fraud should be made directly to the Director-General, who will ensure that all reports are treated confidentially.
   - During project implementation, FAO, as AE, will ensure due monitoring and supervision through country offices.

6. **Fraud Reporting**
   - FAO has established a system for reporting fraud and corruption.
   - The Office of the Controller and Budget (OCB) is responsible for receiving reports of suspected fraud and corruption.
   - In cases where fraud or corruption is suspected, FAO will take appropriate action to investigate and address the issue.

- **Objective 2:** To promote a supportive policy environment for the implementation of mitigation actions at all levels.
- **Objective 3:** To strengthen health systems and their resilience to climate change.

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Green Climate Fund (Proposal seq of 9)

### Introduction

This proposal seeks to formalize arrangements for the management of resources generated by the Authority and Long-term Governance Arrangements (LTGA) for the REDD+ Results-Based Payments (RBP).

On 16 July 2018, Section 5.3 of OCSA, Section 4.1 of the Protocol and Annex I of the Protocol were amended to include a provision that allows the Authority to enter into an agreement with the GCF for the management of RBP. This agreement will be governed by the rules prescribed under Part IX, as modified by the rules prescribed in this proposal.

To that effect, this benefits-sharing plan is specially designed and modeled to ensure that REDD+ Results-Based Payments (RBP) are distributed fairly and transparently. The Authority will be responsible for reporting on the implementation of this proposal.

### Implementation

In this regard, RBP has recently agreed to a set of Guidelines on benefits-sharing and distribution (BSDs) to create a transparent and accountable process for the management of RBP. These guidelines have been developed in consultation with relevant stakeholders.

Beneficiaries may include but are not limited to: communities, the society, and the public sector. Including any other entities that may be identified by the Authority. The benefits-sharing plan will be implemented through a transparent and participatory process. This includes:

- **Participatory processes:** These processes should be carried out in the Benefits Sharing Plan (RBP), ensuring effective monitoring or resource allocation.
- **Transparency:** The plan should include a clear description of how RBP is distributed, including the criteria for eligibility and the process for determining benefits.

### Objectives

The development of a sound, inclusive and participatory RBP plan that avoids excluding vulnerable groups and other potential beneficiaries. This plan will be based on the principles set out in GCF rules to ensure transparency and accountability.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP273

**Proposal name:** Papua New Guinea REBDP plus RBP for results period 2014 - 2016

**Accredited entity:** Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

**Country:** Papua New Guinea

**Project status:** Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### 1.1 Project background

1. At its eighty-sixth meeting (EB 86), the Board decided to adjust up to USD 500,000 from the Regular Programme Budget (RPB) for the implementation of the REBDP plus RBP Programme in Papua New Guinea.
2. At its ninety-third meeting (EB 93), the Board approved the extension of the REBDP plus RBP Programme until December 2016 and decided to include the results of the project in the regular reporting cycle for the period January - June 2017, with an interim review scheduled for December 2016.
3. At its ninety-ninth meeting (EB 99), the Board decided on an exceptional basis to allow the REBDP plus RBP Programme to continue until the end of the results period and to include the results in the regular reporting cycle for the period January - June 2017, subject to the interim review and due diligence to be carried out by the Secretariat.
4. The project presents Papua New Guinea’s REBDP plus results for the period 2014-2016, accredited in October 2013 (CIP) and closed in December 2017 (CIP).
5. The reported expenditure is USD 899,000, which represents 99 percent of the approved budget.
6. Papua New Guinea will use the proceeds from the RBP to support the actions and activities for the period July - December 2017 (INR). This implementation will focus on the active areas, including the following components:
   * Component 1: Institutional arrangements and coordination capacity of national and provincial agricultural research systems.
   * Component 2: Enhanced economic, production and sustainable livelihoods, and food security.

# History of HEIDI

For several decades, Pangea Next Generation has worked diligently to develop and implement innovative solutions for environmental management and sustainable practices. The company's expertise lies in providing comprehensive services that address complex environmental challenges. HEIDI represents their ongoing commitment with a focus on delivering high-quality outcomes through consumer-driven management of the forest ecosystem.

The proposed use of HEIDI is intended to enhance the efficiency and effectiveness of the company’s operations, ensuring that the needs of both the environment and the community are met. The project aims to improve the overall health of the forest ecosystems by implementing sustainable practices that promote long-term growth and development.

## I. Implementation Strategy

The board may wish to consider appointing this funding proposal in accordance with the requirements set forth in the company’s strategic plan. This will ensure that the objectives are met and align with the goals outlined in document GO-18-02-02.

### 1.1. Project Management

The project management team will be responsible for overseeing the implementation of HEIDI. They will work closely with various stakeholders to ensure that the project is completed on time and within budget. The team will also be responsible for monitoring progress and making any necessary adjustments to ensure the success of the project.

### 1.2. Stakeholder Engagement

Stakeholder engagement will be a critical component of the implementation strategy. The company will work closely with local communities, government agencies, and private stakeholders to ensure that their concerns are addressed and that they have a say in the decision-making process. This will help to build trust and ensure that the project is supported by all parties involved.

## II. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 2.1. Impact Potential

The project is likely aligned with the NSF’s (National Science Foundation) mission to encourage innovation and investments in the forest sector through the proposed use of HEIDI. This will help to promote sustainable development, job creation, and economic growth. The project also aligns with the goals of reducing greenhouse gas emissions, promoting low-emission development, and improving air quality. It seeks to reduce land-use change and deforestation while promoting sustainable forest management practices for current and future generations.

### 2.2. Paradigm Shift Potential

The project aims to address the challenges of a rapidly growing rural population and pressure on land resources by promoting sustainable ecosystem development in targeted regions. This will help to improve food security, reduce poverty, and promote sustainable economic growth. The project also seeks to enhance the effectiveness of existing policies and programs related to environmental management and climate change adaptation.

Direct activities will focus on its projects. Successful outcomes will serve as a model for other countries and communities to consider similar approaches. The project will be implemented in accordance with the company’s strategic plan, and the results will be monitored and evaluated to ensure that they meet the expected objectives.

# Understanding REID+ requirements, the depths and intricacies of their requests explored

## The ESMF implementation: a reasonable tool due to its flexibility for the project assigned

The ESMF implementation is a reasonable tool due to its flexibility for the project assigned, consistent with the expectations for REID+ RPP projects as outlined in the REID+ RPP Implementation Guide. This guide provides a framework for implementing the ESMF in RPP projects and includes guidance on how to use the ESMF to support the implementation of activities planned by RPP projects. The ESMF is designed to be flexible and can be adapted to meet the specific needs of each project.

## The proposal is accompanied by an environmental and social management framework

The proposal is accompanied by an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) that outlines the environmental and social risks during the implementation of activities planned by RPP projects. The ESMF is designed to provide a comprehensive overview of the potential impacts of the project, including those related to biodiversity, water resources, and cultural heritage. The ESMF also includes a risk assessment matrix that identifies the key risks associated with the project and outlines the measures that will be taken to mitigate these risks.

## The proposal is classified as category I

The proposal is classified as category I. The ESMF outlines relevant standards, including the International Finance Corporation's Performance Standards (IFC PS) and the World Bank Group's Environmental, Health, and Safety Guidelines (EHS G). The ESMF also includes an overview of relevant implementation with a signed MOU (Memorandum of Understanding) between the project proponent and the local government. The ESMF is designed to provide a comprehensive overview of the potential impacts of the project, including those related to biodiversity, water resources, and cultural heritage.

## The ESMF is consistent with the GCF TUS the REID+ RPP proposals

The ESMF is consistent with the GCF TUS the REID+ RPP proposals, particularly regarding the expectation of properly applying safeguards in line with present standards. It includes a comprehensive overview of the potential impacts of the project, including those related to biodiversity, water resources, and cultural heritage. The ESMF also includes an overview of relevant implementation with a signed MOU (Memorandum of Understanding) between the project proponent and the local government.

## GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESG (Sustainability Program)

The GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESG (Sustainability Program): The IP policy the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESG (Sustainability Program) was signed on the ESMF Compliance of the REID+ RPP proposals. The GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESG (Sustainability Program) was designed to provide a comprehensive overview of the potential impacts of the project, including those related to biodiversity, water resources, and cultural heritage.

the use of peer plans, consultations to Indigenous Peoples are planned to be undertaken prior to the commencement of any project activities. The AE will ensure that all relevant information is collected and considered in the planning process for each project. This includes but is not limited to: the impact on Indigenous Peoples (such as loss of land, resources, or cultural sites), the impact on the environment, and the impact on other stakeholders. A benefits-sharing plan will be established to support the engagement of Indigenous Peoples in the project. For the AE as a whole, the AE has been designed to ensure that it is responsive to the needs of all communities impacted by its activities.

The AE has also been informed by the principles of the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP). The AE will work with the IP Policy, the AE has included guidelines for engagement with Indigenous Peoples. These guidelines include a commitment to: respect and protect the rights of Indigenous Peoples; ensure that all consultations are conducted in accordance with the UNDRIP; and provide support to Indigenous communities to participate in decision-making processes.

### Gender policy

The AE has submitted a gender assessment and a gender action plan (GAP) to the Green Climate Fund. The GAP is aligned with the GCF Gender Policy, which aims to ensure that climate finance benefits women and men equally, and that women and girls have equal access to and control over resources, goods and services.

The AE recognizes that gender equality is an important aspect of sustainable development. The AE has included a commitment to promote gender equality in its activities. This includes but is not limited to: ensuring that all consultations are conducted with both men and women; providing support to women's organizations; and promoting the participation of women in decision-making processes.

The GAP identifies strategic areas of intervention to support the mainstreaming of gender into the AE. The proposed project includes three components: institutional strengthening (Component 1) and capacity building (Component 2). These components have potential for a greater impact on gender equality. The GAP provides specific actions to address these areas, including but not limited to:

- Providing training and support to women's organizations
- Promoting the participation of women in decision-making processes
- Ensuring that all consultations are conducted with both men and women

The AE will work with the IP Policy, the AE has included guidelines for engagement with Indigenous Peoples. These guidelines include a commitment to: respect and protect the rights of Indigenous Peoples; ensure that all consultations are conducted in accordance with the UNDRIP; and provide support to Indigenous communities to participate in decision-making processes.

## 3.3 Risk

### 3.3.1 Accredited entity/accrediting entity capability
- The Government of Papua New Guinea, through the Ministry of Environment, Conservation and Climate Change (MECCC), is working with the GCF to develop a proposal for accreditation.
- The Government of Papua New Guinea will work with the GCF to ensure that the proposal meets the requirements for accreditation.

### 3.3.2 Programme-specific execution risks
1. Risk of double payments: Papua New Guinea is studying its participation in other funding programmes for REDD+ results that are different from those of the GEF.
2. Risk of non-compliance with the GCF's fiduciary standards: The Government of Papua New Guinea will work with the GCF to ensure compliance with these standards.

### 3.3.3 Land tenure
- The Department of Lands & Physical Planning develops and implements comprehensive policies on land, including land tenure.
- The Department of Lands & Physical Planning is working with the Government of Papua New Guinea to develop a national land policy.

### 3.3.4 Disbursement plans
- GCF will disburse the GEF resources to the AF in a single payment.

## 5.3 Compliance risk (medium risk)
- The funding proposal will be implemented exclusively by EAI, acting as both AE and EE.
- The Government of Papua New Guinea will ensure that all activities are carried out in accordance with the approved project document.

## c) To address these risks, FAO has established internal policies, institutional arrangements, and procedures to ensure that its operations are conducted in accordance with sound governance principles, including:

- **Internal control systems** to monitor and ensure the financing of activities and programmes created in the General Programme of Work (GPW).
- **Administrative (civil) and audit services**, and the use of **internal audits** to independently verify the accuracy and completeness of financial transactions.
- **Code of Conduct** to promote integrity, transparency, and accountability, while the presence of a **full board of governors** ensures comprehensive oversight and accountability.
- **Internal audit function** that provides independent assurance on governance processes.
- **Governance arrangements** overseen through higher-level approvals from FAO's governing bodies.

## d) Considering the project's operating context, the controlled environment includes (but is not limited to):

- **National governments** responsible for implementing projects and programmes.
- **FAO's decentralized offices** that provide technical assistance and support to national governments.
- **Project agreements** with national governments and other partners.
- **Internal control systems** in place at FAO headquarters and regional offices.

# Green Climate Fund

## GCF/B.42/07/Add.13

The report includes the following key recommendations aimed at improving accuracy of the FREL/FRL in the future:

- The type-specific per deforestation baseline growth rates for new activities should be updated to reflect the most recent data.
- Improve the determination of forest regrowth in the EF for new activities.
- Increase accuracy in the determination of the biomass of business carbon stocks resulting from different types of land uses.
- Improve the accuracy of the determination of emissions from degradation events consequent to the first occurrence of deforestation or other land use change. Increased accuracy would further increase the accuracy of the FREL/FRL.

# Green Climate Fund

## Independent Fiduciary Review (IFR)

### Background

Independent fiduciaries are elected to oversee performance, according to standards established in Paris New Guidelines and account for 18-25% of total carbon credits issued by the GCF. The IFR is conducted annually by an independent firm and is guided by the [Greenhouse Gas Management Institute](https://ghgmi.org). The IFR provides a rigorous review of the GCF's performance against the [Paris Agreement](https://unfccc.int/process/the-paris-agreement) and the [GCF Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories](https://ghgmi.org).

### Paragraph 12

Beginning in 2015, Paris New Guidelines confirmed that potentially eligible activities include those that reduce emissions by at least 40% per year of land carbon stocks using the conversion factors provided in the [IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories](https://ghgmi.org). The IFR reviewed data, even though the 2015 IPCC Guidelines provide methods to estimate emissions from the soil carbon stock. Paris New Guidelines require that the data provided by the entity be consistent with the methods used in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines. The IFR reviewed the data and found that the data was consistent with the methods used in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines.

### KEYS (LIST) identifies several issues that can render accuracy. Many of these issues were linked to the incomplete entries of the SEF, which was either under implementation at the time or not updated.

- Lack of country-specific and information baseline cannot be used for cross-school and cross-year comparisons.
- In financial reports, items may appear in different places. This makes it difficult to compare like with like.
- The lack of a consistent approach to depreciation means that there is no consistency in the accuracy determination; the extent of forecasted figures included in the SEF for comparison purposes varies from school to school.
- The use of a higher carbon fraction may result in overestimating carbon emissions. This can be avoided by using the same base year for all calculations and ensuring that the same base year is used consistently across all reports.
- Doing the same for different types of equipment, where distinguishing between fixed and portable assets is important, ensures that the accuracy of the data is maintained. It also ensures that all types of equipment are being considered in the analysis.

# Green Climate Fund

## GCF/B.42/03

### Table of Contents

#### 1. Introduction

The last year of the FBR period was completed in 2015. Results are considered for 2014, 2015 and 2016. There is also data from between the start and end of the FBR/FPC technical period and during the period between the previous and present FBR.

#### 2. Results

The results for the FBR period were presented in 2017. The GCF has approved 83 projects, which represent approximately 65% of the total amount approved by the GCF. This is an increase from the 49 projects approved at the end of the previous FBR/FPC technical period.

Chapter 4 of the technical notes for B.42 includes aggregated commitments, including a breakdown of the results by category and by region. The results are presented in terms of the number of projects approved, quantitative uncertainty, uncertainty of the EPs, and an aggregated uncertainty figure. These results have been developed from the properties of submissions to the GCF FBR (as well as for the 2014-2015 and 2016-2017 periods). The results are also presented in terms of the uncertainty against the GCF BFR period.

The results for the FBR technical period are as follows:

- **Approved projects**: 83
- **Total approved amount (USD)**: 4,959,136,200.75
- **Approved amount (USD)**: 3,213,177,450.00
- **Total approved amount (EUR)**: 4,485,000,000.00
- **Approved amount (EUR)**: 2,926,000,000.00

For both technical periods, the uncertainty for aggregated results was under 30% (17.50%) and 80% (14.44%), respectively. However, the uncertainty in the determination results was 14.44% for the previous period and 29.61% for the present period. The uncertainty in the aggregated results is under 30%.

#### 3. Conclusion

The GCF has approved 83 projects, which represent approximately 65% of the total amount approved by the GCF. This is an increase from the 49 projects approved at the end of the previous FBR/FPC technical period.

For both technical periods, the uncertainty for aggregated results was under 30% (17.50%) and 80% (14.44%), respectively. However, the uncertainty in the determination results was 14.44% for the previous period and 29.61% for the present period. The uncertainty in the aggregated results is under 30%.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP273

## Proposal name
Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP for results period 2014 - 2016

## Accredited entity
Forest and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

## Country
Papua New Guinea

## Project size
Medium

## Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### Overview

1. This is the first submission of the funding proposal Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP for results period 2014 - 2016 to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) for accreditation by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF). The accredited entity (AE) is the United Nations Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO).

2. In the context of REDD+, a Results-based Payment refers to payment made for verified emission reductions achieved through the implementation of activities that reduce greenhouse gas emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, and the conservation and sustainable management of forests.

3. The Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP is submitted to the ITAP in accordance with the Decision on Results Management (1/CP.20) adopted by the Conference of the Parties serving as the meeting of the Parties to the Kyoto Protocol (CMP), as well as the Decision on the Development and Implementation of the Readiness Preparation Provisions (R-PP) submitted to the ITAP by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The R-PP contains a summary of the readiness activities undertaken by Papua New Guinea in preparation for the submission of this proposal.

4. The Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP is based on an approved Readiness Preparation Proposal (R-PP) submitted to the FCPF in 2013 and approved in 2014. The R-PP was developed with support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), and the World Bank.

5. The Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP is based on an approved Readiness Preparation Proposal (R-PP) submitted to the FCPF in 2013 and approved in 2014. The R-PP was developed with support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), and the World Bank.

6. The Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP is based on an approved Readiness Preparation Proposal (R-PP) submitted to the FCPF in 2013 and approved in 2014. The R-PP was developed with support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), and the World Bank.

7. The Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP is based on an approved Readiness Preparation Proposal (R-PP) submitted to the FCPF in 2013 and approved in 2014. The R-PP was developed with support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), and the World Bank.

8. The Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP is based on an approved Readiness Preparation Proposal (R-PP) submitted to the FCPF in 2013 and approved in 2014. The R-PP was developed with support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), and the World Bank.

9. The Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP is based on an approved Readiness Preparation Proposal (R-PP) submitted to the FCPF in 2013 and approved in 2014. The R-PP was developed with support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), and the World Bank.

# The sustainable management of forest, and the conservation and enhancement of carbon stocks

The funding proposal is structured around two components: Component 1 aims at increasing the institutional and technical capacities of national and provincial governments in the Pacific region to implement REDD+ activities. This will enhance their ability to monitor and report on the sustainable development of community landowners and local communities in the targeted countries.

## The funding proposal covers in principle West Asia, East Asia, Western Europe, North America, and West and East Africa. It also includes the following countries: Australia, Brazil, Canada, China, India, Indonesia, Japan, Mexico, New Zealand, Norway, Philippines, South Korea, Thailand, United Kingdom, and USA. Countries that may have higher risk of deforestation and forest degradation will be prioritized.

### Component 1 will promote national-level coordination as the principal level of engagement for REDD+ activities. It will also support regional and sub-regional cooperation to ensure the effective implementation of REDD+ activities at the local level, while providing better development outcomes in the more vulnerable countries.

## Why REDD funding proposals to GCF are not required to include a Methodology under 22 conditions

The GCF is designed to be flexible and to allow for the inclusion of innovative approaches. The GCF does not require that all projects be based on an approved methodology. This design is based on NDCs, which are developed by countries themselves. However, the GCF has a set of standardized methodologies that can be used as a guide. These include:

- The IPCC 2006 Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories
- The IPCC 2013 Revised Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories

The IPCC guidelines are designed to provide a framework for the measurement, reporting, and verification of greenhouse gas emissions. They are used by countries to report their emissions to the UNFCCC.

### The structure of the funding proposal demonstrates strong alignment with the action plan developed under the Paris Agreement. It also aligns well with the GCF's strategic priorities, including enhanced transparency, improved environmental integrity, and sustainable development. Specifically, the project will support the implementation of the Paris Agreement by providing financial resources to countries that are working to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions.

### In areas requiring readiness support to GCF, the AF will use the simplified Annual Performance Report (APR) template developed under the GCF Readiness Programme. This template is designed to be simple and easy to use. It includes a set of indicators that can be used to track progress towards the goals of the Paris Agreement.

### The AF will also provide support to countries that are working to develop their own national systems for monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) of greenhouse gas emissions. This support will include technical assistance and capacity building to help countries develop their own MRV systems.

### In order to ensure the effective use of resources, the AF will work closely with the GCF Secretariat to identify priority areas for support. The AF will also provide support to countries that are working to develop their own national systems for monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) of greenhouse gas emissions. This support will include technical assistance and capacity building to help countries develop their own MRV systems.

# 1.4 Pandion shipt potential

## Scale: Medium to High

This is supported by evidence covered by the funding proposal, representing approximately 62 per cent of Paparoa New Zealand's total land area and 80 per cent of its total population. Accounting for the region's high proportion of Maori and Pasifika populations, the potential for economic development is enhanced through the provision of employment opportunities in the tourism and hospitality sectors.

The region has established a strong reputation as an eco-tourism destination, with significant growth in sustainable tourism activities. The region also has a strong history of environmental protection and conservation, particularly in relation to the protection of native flora and fauna. This provides a strong foundation for the development of new eco-tourism opportunities that can be marketed to both domestic and international tourists.

The region's proximity to major centres such as Auckland and Wellington makes it an attractive destination for visitors from these areas. The region also has a high level of accessibility, with a number of major highways and airports providing easy access to the region.

## However...

It is important to note that some major drivers of deforestation in Paparoa New Zealand include the country's high levels of urbanisation and the increasing demand for land for agricultural purposes. The region also has a high level of poverty, which can lead to an increase in illegal logging activities. Additionally, the region has a high level of corruption, which can hinder the development of sustainable tourism initiatives.

# 1.5 Sustainable development potential

## Scale: High

Breaking continuing to climate action, this funding proposal has the potential to contribute to achieving other Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 7 (Affordable and Clean Energy), SDG 9 (Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure) and SDG 13 (Climate Action). Partnerships for the goals are also a key component of this funding proposal.

The region has significant potential to contribute to achieving REEDH objectives in addition, through its unique natural resources. The region's sustainable tourism industry is expected to contribute to enhancing sustainable resource management, by providing an incentive to enhance and protect these resources.

Social and gender equality outcomes can be enhanced by the provision of employment opportunities for women and youth in the tourism sector. This includes the provision of training and education opportunities for those who are currently unemployed or underemployed.

The region also has significant potential to contribute to achieving SDG 1 (No Poverty) through the development of sustainable tourism initiatives that can provide a source of income for local communities. The region's high level of poverty means that there is a significant need for initiatives that can help to address this issue.

# Financial and technical capacity of women and youth or assisting gender-specific needs

The definition of procurement systems and sustainable sourcing practices is essential to ensure that the needs of women and youth are met.

## Environmental co-benefits

By leveraging a streamlined 1.4 billion investment in environmental co-benefits, it is possible to achieve significant reductions in greenhouse gas emissions through the implementation of renewable energy projects and improved energy efficiency measures. This can also help to reduce air pollution and improve public health outcomes.

### Sectoral Co-benefits

The sectoral co-benefits of financing critical infrastructure have been carbon benefits that have been identified under the carbon benefits from the implementation of NHR, which includes the area of carbon benefits from sustainable forest products.

## Needs of the recipient

Papua New Guinea is classified as a lower-middle-income country with a population nearing 11 million and a gross domestic product (GDP) of USD 27.3 billion in 2021. The country faces significant challenges, including high levels of poverty, inequality, and unemployment. These challenges are exacerbated by the impacts of climate change, which threaten the sustainability of the natural resources upon which the economy depends.

According to the latest reports from the International Monetary Fund (IMF) and the World Bank (WB), Papua New Guinea is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to natural disasters. The World Bank Global Adaptation Initiative ranks Papua New Guinea as the 10th most vulnerable country to climate change, with high vulnerability to natural hazards. In this context, the WB has emphasized the importance of investing in climate resilience and adaptation measures.

The financial package will support the government's priority areas, including new energy projects that can help reduce greenhouse gas emissions and improve access to renewable energy sources. These investments are among the major barriers that can slow down growth and create more barriers to sustainable development.

**Page 144**
---

# Country oversight

## Scale: High

The funding proposal includes an advisory letter issued by the Climate Change and Development Authority (CCDA), the National Designated Authority to GCF, dated December 2024.

Papua New Guinea submitted its second Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) in accordance with the Paris Agreement. The NDC aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 30% below business-as-usual levels by 2030 through a combination of mitigation and adaptation measures. The GCF funding will support the implementation of these measures.

The proposal aligns with Papua New Guinea's National REDD+ Strategy (NRSS) and will be based on differing methodologies, all nationally appropriate mitigation actions (NAMAs), and the UNFCCC and the Green Climate Fund (GCF).

## Efficiency and effectiveness

### Scale: High

The funding proposal reports a total-based payment of USD 4.11 LT for emission reductions achieved in 2030.

The funding proposal is based on the results of the REDD+ project, including a set of rigorous safeguards, the progress towards the project's objectives, and the effectiveness of the measures implemented to achieve these objectives.

FAB has been recognized that occurs in managing developing countries in the implementation of the NDC. The FAB will be supported by the GCF and the Climate Change and Development Authority (CCDA) to ensure the efficient use of resources and the achievement of national targets and priorities.

The funding proposal is based on the country's oversight of the funding proposal as high.

# achieving and aligning visions to achieve greater environmental impact on archipelago carbon and climate finance

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is committed to providing resources to developing countries to address climate change. The GCF aims to promote a paradigm shift in the way climate change actions are funded, with an emphasis on results-based approaches that balance effectiveness and equity. Providing funds with proven success in reducing GHG emissions, enhancing carbon sinks, and building resilience can help achieve these goals. Furthermore, aligning projects with existing REDD+ solutions will increase priority allocations.

In Papua New Guinea (PNG), the GCF has supported several initiatives to enhance climate resilience and reduce greenhouse gas emissions. These efforts have been aligned with the country's commitment to reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, enhancing carbon stocks, and conserving biodiversity.

1. **Future Projects:**
   - Future projects will leverage synergies with existing REDD+ initiatives in PNG.
   - The GCF will continue to support projects that align with the EU-REDD+ Programme and the UNDP-GEF Small Grants Programme (SGP).
   - Future projects will focus on building local capacity, promoting indigenous knowledge, and enhancing community engagement.

2. **Funding Priorities:**
   - The GCF will identify priority areas for funding based on the needs of local communities.
   - Funding will be directed towards projects that promote sustainable land use practices, including agroforestry and integrated landscape management.
   - The GCF will also support projects that enhance community resilience to climate change.

3. **Capacity Building:**
   - The GCF will provide training and technical assistance to build the capacity of local communities to implement REDD+ initiatives.
   - Funding will be provided for projects that promote sustainable livelihoods, including ecotourism and sustainable agriculture.
   - The GCF will also support projects that enhance community engagement in decision-making processes.

## II. Overall remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

a. **The funding proposal for Papua New Guinea's REDD+ BIP for results-based payment 2014-2018** (reference number: GCF/B.09/36) was approved by the Board in 2015, representing a program of USD 61.417.58 for 16,969.26 tCO2e of emission reductions achieved over five years.
   - The proposal was developed with the assistance of the UNDP-GEF Small Grants Programme (SGP) and the World Bank's Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF).
   - The GCF Board approved the program in 2015, with a total budget of USD 61.417.58.

b. **The Independent Technical Advisory Panel** (ITAP) reviewed the proposal and provided recommendations to the GCF Board.

| 2018 | 4/6/2018      | GEF/APF/2018/32 | 7,516,637  |
| **TOTAL** |              |                | **23,324,955** |

### Description

Section 10 of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) modified procedures for accreditation and funding provides that the GCF will support an entity as a constitutive implementing agency well as an executing entity. The GCF will also consider accredited implementing entities as co-executing agencies.

From this Chapter 7 for the first time includes the methodology for the calculation of the results-based payments (RBPs) for the project ID: 013. The RBP methodology is based on the following criteria:

- **Indicator**: RBP (2015, 2016, 2017 and 2018)
- **Threshold**: RBP (2015, 2016, 2017 and 2018): 4.3%
- **Baseline**: RBP (2015, 2016, 2017 and 2018): 4.3%

The calculation of the RBPs is based on the preparation using the following formula:

\[ \text{RBP} = \frac{\text{Indicator}}{\text{Baseline}} \]

# 1. Introduction

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) recognizes the central importance of gender considerations as terms of both impact and access to climate funding. Consistent with the GCF's Terms of Reference for Gender Policy, the GCF has developed a Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP) to ensure that gender equality is integrated into all aspects of its operations. This includes the development of gender-specific assessments, indicators, and actions to support the implementation of the GCF's Gender Policy (2015) and the Addendum for Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities (IPLCs).

The GEAP aims to promote gender equality and empower women by addressing the needs of vulnerable groups, including women, youth, indigenous peoples, persons with disabilities, and LGBTIQ+ individuals. It also seeks to enhance gender mainstreaming in GCF-funded projects and programs.

The purpose of the Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAAP) is to provide a comprehensive overview related to the role of women in the development and management of climate change adaptation and mitigation activities. The GAAP will support the Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+) project by ensuring women's participation in REDD+ activities, including their involvement in decision-making processes and benefit-sharing mechanisms.

The GAAP is aligned with the GCF's National Gender Policy and benefits from the experience of the GCF's National Gender Policy and benefits from the experience of the GCF's National Gender Policy and ### 1.2 Brief Overview of the Proposed RDD+ Project

The proposed RDD+ Project has been developed under the umbrella of PNG’s National REDD+ strategy (NDC) and is thus positioned to directly contribute to the implementation of selected activities in the NDC. The project will also support the Government of Papua New Guinea’s (GoPNG) commitment to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation as well as the sustainable management of forests, conservation of forest carbon stocks, and enhancement of forest carbon stocks through afforestation and reforestation. The project will also provide long-term incentives for activities relating to the conservation and sustainable use of all types of forests and enhance management systems required to ensure transparency of results delivered and the effectiveness of actions.

The RDD+ Project is designed to be implemented in the provinces of Madang, Morobe, East Sepik and the targeted provinces of the NDC. The project will focus on the following outcomes:

- **Outcome 1:** Establish the National Sustainable Land Use Planning (NSLUP) framework for the national and provincial governments.
- **Outcome 2:** Develop a sustainable land use planning system that is integrated with management, promotion, and local implementation for forest sectors and local communities.
- **Outcome 3:** Support the implementation of the NDC, NRS, NDC for the CCO.

### OUTCOMES

- **Outcome 1:** More government institutions at the national and provincial level has improved the capacity to implement the NSLUP framework.
- **Outcome 2:** The sustainable land use planning system is integrated with management, promotion, and local implementation for forest sectors and local communities.
- **Outcome 3:** Support the implementation of the NDC, NRS, NDC for the CCO.

PNG is also to benefit from the UNFCCC regarding the implementation of the NDC and the NRS.

# THE GENDER ASSESSMENT (GA)

## 2. EXISTING GENDER DYNAMICS IN PNG

Over the last 20 years, PNG has made some strides in promoting gender equality within the country and its tally actions to promote and integrate gender equality into the broader policy making process. The Government of Papua New Guinea (GoPNG) has developed a Gender Equality Policy (GEP) which is currently being reviewed by the National Executive Council (NEC). The GEP, along with other policies such as the National Gender Equality Commission Act 2013 and the National Policy on Women in Development (NPWD), provides a framework for gender equality. The Government has also established a Gender Equality Commission to oversee the implementation of these policies.

### 2.1 Gender Inequality Indexes

Several indices have been developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality and to allow comparisons over time and across countries. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) Human Development Report (HDR) 2014 Gender Inequality Index (GII) is one such index. The GII measures gender inequalities in three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment, and economic participation and opportunity. The GII ranges from 0 to 1, with 0 indicating that men and women are equal in all dimensions and 1 indicating the maximum inequality. PNG has a GII value of 0.586, ranking it 133rd out of 187 countries in the world.

The Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) is another tool that measures the gap between men and women in four key areas: economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment. The GGGI ranges from 0 to 1, with 0 indicating parity and 1 indicating inequality. PNG has a GGGI value of 0.638, ranking it 142nd out of 152 countries in the world.

### 2.2 Education

Papua New Guinea (PNG) has achieved gender parity in primary school enrolment, but disparities widen as students move through higher levels of education. While boys and girls have equal access to primary schools, girls are more likely to drop out of secondary school due to early marriage, pregnancy, and cultural norms that discourage women from pursuing further education. Girls also face significant challenges in accessing tertiary education.

The National Education Sector Plan (NESP) 2015-2019 aims to address these issues and promote gender equality in education. The plan includes targets for increasing the number of girls in secondary schools, improving the quality of education for all students, and the participation of women in decision-making processes at all levels.

The National Policy on Women in Development (NPWD) also aims to promote gender equality in education by addressing the barriers that prevent women from accessing and completing their education. The policy includes targets for increasing the number of girls in secondary schools, improving the quality of education for all students, and the participation of women in decision-making processes at all levels.

Girls often leave school early due to marry, concerns, financial constraints, inadequate educational and economic opportunities, and social practices that prefer boys' education over girls'. Girls who are married at an early age often drop out of school. The impact of early marriage on girls' education is profound; it affects their health, household income, and life expectancy. Early marriages also undermine the girl's ability to complete her education.

Poor school environments further contribute to these dropout rates. Many girls report facing harassment in schools, including sexual abuse by teachers and other students. Some schools lack adequate facilities such as proper toilets, which forces girls to drop out due to safety concerns. Additionally, schools often lack proper sanitation facilities, making it difficult for girls to manage their menstrual hygiene.

Girls' education is also hindered by cultural practices that value boys' education over girls'. Rural girls face additional pressures, including higher costs of school fees and uniforms. Furthermore, many families prioritize boys' education due to societal norms and expectations. Girls often have more household responsibilities, which limit their time for studying and attending school.

### 2.3 Political Participation and Decision-Making

Women's political participation and representation is extremely weak in PHC with none of the 111 elected representatives being women. Out of a total of 3600 candidates, only two were women. This highlights the persistent gender disparity in political representation.

Female participation in politics has been historically low. Since 1977, the first time a woman was elected to any office was in 2015 when a woman was elected as a member of the provincial assembly. The number of female representatives in the 2017 elections increased slightly but remains minimal.

At a national level, the situation is equally dismal. Women's representation in parliament is low and has not improved significantly over time. Despite some progress, women still face significant barriers to political participation, including lack of support from their families and communities.

The government has taken steps to address this issue. The Ministry of Parliamentary Affairs has introduced a quota system for women in local government bodies. This was followed by the Government's decision to reserve 33% of seats for women in local government elections. Additionally, the Government has established a Gender Equality Unit within the Cabinet Secretariat to oversee the implementation of these policies.

The integration of gender equality into national and local level government elections is a significant step forward. However, more needs to be done to ensure that women have equal opportunities to participate in political processes and decision-making.

In relation to land held under customary tenure, women have little to no control over it; if they do it is held in the name of the man (the head of the household) and most of the control lies with him. The lack of control over land means that women are unable to use it as collateral for loans or credit facilities to purchase inputs such as seeds, fertilizer, tools or cattle. Women also have little say in the planning of rural development projects such as irrigation schemes, roads, schools and health centers. This is exacerbated by the fact that women are often excluded from decision-making bodies such as village councils and land committees. When plantations of tea, coffee and semi-permanent crops are planted on customary land, women are often excluded from participation in these activities.

It is important to note that the lack of control over land means that women have little say in the planning and management of their households. This has a significant impact on their ability to participate in income-generating activities such as farming, micro-enterprise or employment outside the home. Women are also often excluded from decision-making bodies such as village councils and land committees.

People involved in using land to which they do not have a customary right of ownership or a customary right of occupancy must obtain permission from the owner or occupier before they can use it. This is particularly important for people who are involved in using land for income-generating activities such as farming, micro-enterprise or employment outside the home.

The Land Act (1998) includes provisions related to customary land, such as regulations that do not affect other tenures, such as the recognition of unregistered customary land and women's rights. The Act also provides for the registration of customary land and the protection of women's rights in relation to land ownership. Women often face challenges in enforcing their rights to land and property or in obtaining some form of security of tenure.

## Gender-Based Violence and Access to Social Resources

Including partner violence (physical and sexual abuse), it nearly twice the global average in Papua New Guinea (PNG). According to the latest Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) 2016-17, 34% of women aged between 15 and 49 years reported experiencing emotional abuse about their husbands, in-laws or public-humiliation physical violence, and sexual violence. The prevalence is higher among women living in rural areas.

The impact of gender-based violence on women's health is well documented. It has been shown to have a negative effect on the reproductive health of women, including increased risk of sexually transmitted infections (STIs), unintended pregnancies, miscarriages, and stillbirths. The psychological consequences of such violence can lead to depression, anxiety, post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), and other mental health issues.

Gender-based violence is not just a women's issue; it affects men as well. Men who experience violence are more likely to perpetrate violence against their partners and children. This intergenerational cycle of violence can have a lasting impact on individuals, families, and communities.

On average, 1 in 3 women (33%) of the total number of women aged between 15 and 49 years reported experiencing physical or sexual violence by an intimate partner at some point in their lifetime. On average, each woman member has 1.16 acts of work per year as a result of the violence they have experienced.

- 23% of women (23%) of the total number of women aged between 15 and 49 years reported experiencing physical or sexual violence by an intimate partner at some point in their lifetime.
- On average, each woman member has 1.16 acts of work per year as a result of the violence they have experienced.

Different countries have been able to align to address this issue, including the development of the National Strategy to Prevent and Respond to Gender-Based Violence, 2017-2021. The strategy aims to promote gender equality and social justice, and to provide support and advocacy for survivors, as well as to prevent violence.

# The National Central OCD (OCD) Governance has been approached for political party through a coalition of Governors, committing themselves to bring action towards a release under legal validity and to promote gender equality, respect and partnership.

In addition to these initiatives, NCDC established Gender Desks in the city as part of a partnership with the National Democratic Institute (NDI) to support women's participation in local government. The Gender Desks are designed to address issues such as the project also aimed at enhancing women's participation in local governance and development targeting women and youth.

## Social Services

In terms of access to social services, the Government of RMI through both national, provincial and district levels has been working with various partners including non-governmental organizations (NGOs) to ensure that all citizens have access to basic services. This includes providing support for women in rural areas, as well as those within the community.

### Community Health Services

Most lay and traditional village courts are handed by local or district police and referred to village courts. These courts handle minor cases such as land disputes, family matters, and other issues that are handled by the formal legal system, e.g., the Police of the village courts. The village courts also provide services in areas such as health, education, and social welfare (e.g., many use locally organized community-based health insurance schemes). NCDC and NGOs work together to support these efforts.

### 2.7 Gender and Agriculture

The economic growth of Papua New Guinea is driven by the agricultural sector, which conflicts with the traditional role of women in agriculture. Women are responsible for most of the work on rural areas. The real economy, whether is predominantly comprised of subsistence and small-scale farming.

In the highlands, agriculture labor is strongly defined by gender roles. Men are responsible for clearing land, building houses and fences, and maintaining tools. Women are responsible for planting crops, weeding, harvesting and processing food. This division of labor is reinforced by the fact that women have less access to education and training than men. The highest levels of violence against women occur in rural areas where women are more likely to be involved in agriculture.

Women's participation in agricultural production is also limited by their lack of access to credit, land and other resources. They are also more vulnerable to exploitation and abuse because they have less control over the means of production.

Building on this, the ILO-PHR Human Affair Office is continuing and preparing the other are needed, and your IDM have performed with the PHR department of justice and the Department of Labor. The ILO has been working on the development of a program for the promotion of international labor standards and the protection of workers' rights. This includes the development of a program to promote the rights of indigenous peoples, including their right to land and resources.
