the communal culture and health care needs of the family are fulfilled, in addition to the community, social and cultural needs they are required to perform. Given these multiple roles and responsibilities, women's health is often compromised. Women that cook provide food for their families, and all family members eat the same food. Women also bear the responsibility of reproduction and rearing of household members as children are considered a wealth asset, as well as an investment in the future.

Those living in highly marginal environments suffer from periodic food shortage. As a buffer against such risks, production of staple food crops such as sweet potatoes, peanuts, yams, and cassava is practiced year-round by women. Women also engage in gathering wild foods and medicinal plants to supplement their diets. Women are responsible for most of the planting, weeding, and harvesting of these crops.

The use of cash sold crops is local or urban markets generates cash for women and their families. The sale of these crops provides income that can be used to purchase food, clothing, school fees, and other necessities. Women in rural areas are often responsible for long-distance marketing, with a basket headload produce to Port Moresby or Lae markets to sell. In some cases, women travel alone to distant markets to market their cash crops. The use of cash income is an important source of empowerment for women. Women also engage in various remunerating activities such as weaving, carving, and selling handicrafts. Households in PNG generate a great deal more income from the sale of these products than from the sale of agricultural produce. This has led to greater adoption of improved farming and irrigation methods. It is largely supported.

### Gender and health finance

There are important gender specific farmers to acquiring finance or extension services, as women often lack access to credit and other financial resources required for investment in agriculture. Understanding the economic performance of women is key to improving the large-scale agricultural sector. Women's participation in the agricultural sector is crucial to the development of the country. Women play a vital role in the production of food crops (WFP). Women are also involved in the marketing of cash crops and other products. Women are responsible for the majority of the planting, weeding, and harvesting of these crops.

### Access to financial resources

Access to financial resources is essential for fostering sustainable agriculture and ensuring food security. A number of Financial Services (FSPs) financial services conducted by the Bank of Papua New Guinea (BPNG) have been established to provide access to credit and other financial products, particularly for women and individuals in rural areas. These FSPs are designed to address the challenges in obtaining credit and financial products, particularly for women and individuals in remote areas.

# POLICY AND REGULATORY FRAMEWORK PROTECTING WOMEN AND GENDER EQUALITY

## Key International Protocols and Frameworks Supporting Gender Equality, Women's Empowerment, and Human Rights

PNG has signed and ratified various international conventions and treaties on human rights, social inclusion and equality, some of which have had their laws amended to promote gender equality and women’s empowerment. These include:

### Table 1: List of Relevant International Treaties Signed or Ratified by PNG

| **Document/ Treaty** | **Ratification** |
|---------------------|------------------|
| UD Declaration on Human Rights | Not Available |
| Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination | 1995 |
| Convention against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment | 2002 |
| The Convention on the Rights of the Child | 1991 |
| The International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination | 1996 |
| The Optional Protocol to the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights | Not Available |

### Begin Key Declaration and Platform for Action

Other global and regional initiatives in gender equality. JAERN’s endorsement such as the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action signed during the Fourth World Conference on Women in Beijing, China in 1995. This declaration was a landmark event that recognized the need to achieve gender equality globally and to eliminate all forms of discrimination against women. The platform outlined strategic objectives and actions to promote gender equality and empower women.

As a result of this international platform and plan of action, PNG chose to include the development of a National Gender Equality Policy in 2013. This policy was developed through extensive consultations with key stakeholders and is instrumental in promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment. The policy also includes a framework for monitoring and evaluating progress towards achieving gender equality.

### Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) Implementation & Progress in PNG

As part of the implementation of the United Nations gender equality goals in PNG, PNG is following up on the SDG 5 which focuses on Gender Equality. This goal aims to achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls.

# Partnership between the ILO and the Government of PNG in support of the 2030 Agenda

PNG adopted the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development and the Small Island Developing State (SIDS) Accelerated Modalities of Action (SAMOA Pathway) at the United Nations General Assembly in September 2015. The Government of PNG has committed to implementing these international agreements until key milestones such as the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) are achieved by 2030 and also aligned on-the-MFDP by 2021 with other national policies.

Progress has been challenging for many of the thematic areas particularly to gender equality due to institutional constraints. However, the ILO Visitors National Review Report (VNR) provides a comprehensive overview of the progress made towards achieving the SDGs and the specific actions that relate to Goal 5 on Gender Equality. The most notable are covered below.

## Progress in Gender Equality

PNG has signed up to a number of progressive international conventions concerning women's rights, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Optional Protocol to CEDAW. PNG has also ratified the following:

- The National Family Health and Violence Action Committee, several committees under CMAC, operating at both provincial and district levels, playing a role in coordinating practical action data.
- Certain government departments, example: the National Department of Health is leading the implementation of the National Strategy to Prevent and Respond to Gender-Based Violence (GBV).
- The policy established the first Family and Sexual Violence Unit (FSVU) in 2016 growing to 3 units by 2019.
- The Office of the Public Prosecutor is operating a Special Unit Officer, which in one time has been able to prosecute cases of sexual violence.
- Partnership also passed the family Protection Act in 2013.
- The number of women in parliament increased from 7 in 2012 to 22 in 2017. The overall parliamentary representation improved from 7% in 2008 to 45 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2019. Eighty-seven percent of women have been able to access health services and 31 percent respectively of defined a healthy facility as compared with 51 percent and 51 percent respectively.
- Regarding education, the gender parity rate for elementary school was recorded at 1.07.
- The number of girls in secondary schools has increased from 46% in 2008 to 53% in 2019.
- PNG is a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Optional Protocol to CEDAW. PNG has also ratified the following:

# 2. National Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment Frameworks, Policies and Laws

## 3.1 National Gender Laws and Regulations

Human rights and special rights that promote gender equality and non-discrimination have been ratified by the Government of PNG and enshrined within the Constitution of Papua New Guinea (PNG). These include the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Convention on the Rights of the Child. The government has also enacted legislation to promote gender equality, including the Family Protection Act 1971, Domestic Violence Act 2013, Sexual Offences Act 2013, and the National Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Policy.

Apart from adopting the legal needs of women who are considered more vulnerable to issues of abandonment and domestic violence within the context of marriage or relationships, the government has also taken steps to address gender-based violence. The Family Protection Law 2017 resulted in various amendments to the Criminal Code of Papua New Guinea (PNG) to include offenses such as marital rape, sexual coercion, and other forms of abuse. These laws are further developed to include giving consideration to the circumstances of men, as well as women, who experience domestic violence. The government has also established a National Committee on Violence against Women and Girls.

In addition to these actions in legislation, current policies and measures to address the special needs of women and girls include:

- The Office of the Public Prosecutor instituted Victim Liaison Officers, which in law are provided with the Family and Sexual Violence Act.
- The government has established a National Committee on Gender-Based Violence under the Department of Community Development (DCD), which provides policy direction to address gender-based violence.
- Certain government departments have also implemented policies. For example, the National Department of Health has a policy that addresses gender equality in its operations.
- SOPHNG aims to annually empower 50 percent of all women under the Mothers’ Health Initiative.

## 3.2 Gender Equality and Reproductive Health Laws

Gender equality and reproductive health have been recognized as important issues in the past 10 years and continue to be a priority for the government. The government has established policies, programs, and strategies that aim to promote gender equality and reproductive health. These include:

- Ensuring access to reproductive health services.
- Promoting gender equality in all aspects of life.
- Addressing the needs of women and girls in terms of their reproductive health.

These efforts are part of a broader strategy to improve the overall well-being of women and girls, including their access to education, employment, and other opportunities. The government has also implemented policies that aim to promote gender equality in the workplace and to address the specific needs of women and girls.


---
**Page 185**
---

Although the Family Act 1997 enacted 18th-century legislation, the rights of women in decision-making over the management of family resources through women representation on the household level still remain a challenge. Women's empowerment is a prerequisite to achieving gender equality. There are specific references to equality for women and men in relation to the Family Law Act and Education Amendment (the latter specifically referring to equal pay). The Family Law Act also provides for the establishment of family dispute resolution services and the provision of legal aid for women who are victims of domestic violence.

Despite these general gains, women's institutions have continued to address the issue of gender inequality in recent years. Women's organizations have been at the forefront of advocating for women's rights and have played a significant role in promoting women's participation in decision-making processes. These organizations have also worked to raise awareness about health issues such as sexually transmitted diseases (STDs), reproductive health, and family planning. They have advocated for women's rights through training sessions on budgeting and savings through engagement with banks.

The government has also taken steps to address gender inequality by implementing policies that promote equal opportunities in the workplace. These include measures to ensure equal pay for men and women, as well as initiatives to increase the number of women in leadership positions. The government has also established a Gender Equality Unit within the Ministry of Women's Affairs to coordinate and implement these policies.

In addition to these efforts, the government has also taken steps to promote gender equality through legislation. For example, the Employment Act 2015 provides for equal pay for men and women in the workplace. The government has also established a Gender Equality Committee within the Ministry of Women's Affairs to oversee the implementation of these policies.

The government has also taken steps to address the issue of gender-based violence by establishing a National Gender Policy. This policy aims to promote equality between men and women, as well as to provide support for victims of domestic violence. The government has also established a number of shelters for women who are victims of domestic violence.

In addition to these efforts, the government has also taken steps to address the issue of gender-based discrimination by establishing a National Gender Policy. This policy aims to promote equality between men and women, as well as to provide support for victims of domestic violence. The government has also established a number of shelters for women who are victims of domestic violence.

### 1.2.3 National Gender Policies, Plans and Programs

Many of the key gender goals and plans in the country come into effect within the [ODD+] period including the Papua New Guinea National Policy for Women and Gender Equity 2017-2035 which aims to promote equality between women and men within the service and across all sectors of government. The Policy describes the government’s mission to promote gender-equitable development, participation, and empowerment of women and men in all aspects of life. This includes ensuring that women have equal access to services and opportunities equally into the future.

While gender mainstreaming across government has resulted in some policies and strategies, the lack of a national policy framework for gender integration in PNG has limited the ability to effectively implement these initiatives. The National Strategy for Gender Equality 2010-2020 remains the key guiding framework for gender integration in PNG. There is an urgent need to develop a new national strategy that reflects the current context.

The government has been working with different organizations to ensure that women are empowered and have access to resources.

### 3.2.4 Gender institutions and legislation: Decision making

National Council of Women (NCW) was the first national body established by the Government in 1979 to represent the interests of women and children in PMC and to give advice to the rights of women and children. The NCW has been instrumental in the development of laws and policies that affect women and children.

Shortly after the establishment of the NCW, government departments were formed to further enhance the empowerment of women and children. These include the Department of Women's Affairs and the Department of Children. The Department of Women's Affairs was established in 1980 and the Department of Children was established in July 2000. The latter being responsible for the development of policies and programs that promote the well-being of children. The coordination with key line agencies and monitoring of achievements is essential to sustainable development.

Both women and men are able to bring the issue of gender equality in PMC because women's concerns link in sufficient lobbying by the government for the implementation of their various gender equality initiatives. This has been further enhanced through partnerships with the Department of Community Development and the Constitutional and Legal Reforms Commission. The latter is responsible for the promotion and protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms.

Apart from the government funded authorities there are also many NGOs and private sector organizations that have been working on issues affecting women and children. The situation has improved where the central RCD and key regional agencies are now fully engaged in the implementation of gender equality initiatives.

In addition to greater and concerted change, the Climate Change Management Act 2015 provides for a woman representative on the Board of the Climate Change Development Authority (CCDA) and the National Climate Change Committee. The Climate Change Act 2016 which is presently being incorporated into a national policy also includes provisions for women's participation.

# 12.4 Gender and Climate Change Policies

Policy NDC emphasizes the crucial role of the IUA6 (Land Use, Land-Use Change, and Forestry) sector in emissions from agriculture and forestry sectors, highlighting the need for gender-responsive policies to enhance women's and men's roles in the implementation of sustainable agricultural and agronomic practices.

- Promoting socially inclusive climate smart agriculture
  - Enhance women's access to resources, information, and climate smart technologies.
  - Encourage women's participation in decision-making processes related to agriculture.
  - Promote gender fair rehabilitation or sustainable land management.

The NDC also highlights the importance of a gender-responsive and inclusive approach, ensuring that policies are designed to promote gender equality. This approach seeks to promote multiple benefits for sustainable development, including the conservation of biodiversity, reducing the human rights abuses associated with climate change, and enhancing women's empowerment.

Further to this, the Climate Change Management Act 2017 provides for women's participation on key decision-making forums such as the Board of the GCCA and in various technical Working Groups. The Act also provides for the establishment of a Gender Equality Unit within the Ministry of Environment, which was established in 2014, primarily to promote gender equality policy.

The Fourth Comprehensive Development Management Plan of 2014 also mentions the need for "fair and equitable access to resources" as well as to promote women's empowerment. As mentioned above, GCCA applies Guidelines for Gender Equality in the Workplace.

There was a reference already made to the Family Policy 1990 and its promotion of the equality of rights between men and women. The policy also promotes equal opportunities for both genders at home and in the workplace.

At the same time, legally binding norms, voluntarily applying and enforcing the new laws and policies must reflect and reinforce such norms as a cornerstone for the Gender Equality Unit to promote gender equality.

# GENDER ASPECTS OF FORESTS IN PNG AND GENDER MAINSTREAMING IN REDD+ ACTIONS

## Women and gender in PNG

The forests of PNG cover approximately 71.8 percent of its total land area, which is about 31,892,415 hectares of land. Both women and men in traditional areas of Papua New Guinea (PNG) rely on forest resources for their livelihoods. Women are primarily engaged in forest-based activities such as collecting fuelwood, medicinal plants, and food for domestic consumption. They also play a significant role in managing agricultural lands and maintaining household gardens.

Women and youth play an important role in management and production of forest land, although they do not have equal access to resources or decision-making processes. When customary land rights are recognized, women tend not to be the primary beneficiaries because their access is often mediated through male relatives. However, when women's access to forests are to meet household needs for fuel, timber, medicinal plants, and food, they can play a significant role in generating opportunities and enterprises.

The broader results in PNG indicates approximately 9525.138 million per annum according to the study conducted by the World Bank. This is about 6.7% of total revenue generated from forestry activities. The contribution of women in the forest sector is significant, but their participation is often limited to unpaid and informal work.

The forests also act as a source of income for rural communities, with households earning some money through sales of timber, firewood, and non-timber forest products (NTFPs). Women are particularly active in collecting NTFPs such as fruits, medicinal plants, and food. They also play an important role in processing these products into marketable goods.

In some regions, women's groups serve as a simple food source and income cooperative effort to sell their produce at local markets. In other areas, they have formed cooperatives to process and market NTFPs such as honey, beeswax, and essential oils. The sale of these products can provide significant additional income for households.

## Gender mainstreaming in REDD+

Gender mainstreaming is the process of assessing the implications for women and men of any planned action, including legislation, policies or programs, in all areas and at all levels. It is a strategy for making the concerns and experiences of women and men an integral dimension of the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of policies and programs so that both women and men benefit equally.

### Gender mainstreaming in REDD+ activities

The integration of gender into REDD+ activities involves several steps:

1. **Assessment**: Identify the gender dimensions of REDD+ activities.
2. **Planning**: Develop strategies to address identified gender issues.
3. **Implementation**: Ensure that both women and men have equal access to benefits from REDD+ activities.
4. **Monitoring and Evaluation**: Track progress towards gender equality goals.

### Challenges in Gender Mainstreaming

Despite the importance of gender mainstreaming, there are several challenges:

1. **Lack of Data**: Limited data on gender-specific impacts of REDD+ activities.
2. **Institutional Barriers**: Lack of institutional support for gender mainstreaming.
3. **Cultural Norms**: Cultural norms that restrict women's participation in decision-making processes.

### Conclusion

Gender mainstreaming is crucial for ensuring that both women and men benefit from REDD+ activities. By addressing the specific needs and challenges faced by women, it is possible to enhance the effectiveness of REDD+ initiatives and contribute to sustainable forest management.

# Efforts to mainstream gender in REDD+

## Gender in the National REDD+ Strategy

In 2019, Peru’s National REDD+ Strategy (NRPS) was approved by the Government[^1], following an extensive process of consultations and participatory workshops. The strategy includes a specific section on gender, which aims to ensure that the benefits of REDD+ are equitably distributed among women and men.

The NRPS is the guiding document for REDD+, a PNG and it is structured in two key components: (i) the strategic component, which defines the vision, objectives, principles, and guidelines; and (ii) the operational component, which includes the necessary development actions providing long-term sustainable benefits for communities. The strategy also establishes the need to ensure that gender issues are addressed in the design and implementation of REDD+ activities.

The NRPS was developed with the support of the National Commission on Women and Family (CONAMU), which is responsible for promoting women’s participation in economic activities and national development plans, and provides that the Ministry of Environment (MINAM) should ensure that gender issues are addressed. Given this collaboration with CONAMU, gender equality is addressed and promoted in the NRPS implementation.

[^1]: MRE Decision Decree UA-2019.

# Transparency International PMI REDD+ Risk Assessment (2014)

Between 2012 and 2014, Transparency International PMI (TI-PMI) conducted total research into the risks of corruption in REDD+ funding and finance. This was significantly related to weak governance systems and lack of transparency, accountability, and participation.

As part of this major focus, a detailed key analysis was carried out on the potential risks and challenges associated with the implementation of REDD+ activities. The assessment included an analysis of the risks and challenges in the implementation of REDD+ activities, including the risks related to weak governance systems and lack of transparency, accountability, and participation.

The assessment strongly emphasized that implementing agencies (IPPC) possess a crucial role in ensuring that the funds are used effectively and efficiently. It also highlighted the need for strong risk management strategies and mechanisms to protect the interests of these vulnerable groups, with particular attention to women and indigenous peoples.

## Success of REDD+ (2017)

Between 2016 and 2017 an intensive study was carried out on gender and REDD+, including the impact of gender equality in the implementation of REDD+. This was conducted by TI-PMI in collaboration with local communities, indigenous peoples, and other stakeholders. The success of REDD+ is dependent on the ability to address the needs of all groups within society, including women and men.

The study included an analysis of the risks and challenges associated with the implementation of REDD+, as well as the potential benefits and opportunities for the development of gender equality in REDD+. It also highlighted the need for a comprehensive approach to addressing the risks of corruption in REDD+ funding and finance, and the importance of ensuring that the rights of women are protected.

The study concluded that the success of REDD+ is dependent on the ability to address the needs of all groups within society, including women and men. It also highlighted the need for a comprehensive approach to addressing the risks of corruption in REDD+ funding and finance, and the importance of ensuring that the rights of women are protected.

# R2BD+ Gender Mainstreaming

The R2BD+ Gender Mainstreaming aimed to enhance the participation of gender equality in R2BD+ programs, actions, and activities. The Government's initiative sought to include gender equality as a cross-cutting issue in all R2BD+ programs.

- **Gender Mainstreaming:** Gender mainstreaming was incorporated into the R2BD+ program and activities. As a result, the R2BD+ strategy, M&E, and proposed activities were designed to promote gender equality.
- **Gender Analysis:** Gender analysis was conducted to identify issues related to gender inequality in the community.
- **Capacity Building:** Capacity-building programs were implemented to enhance the capacity of local governments to address gender-related issues.

## Mainstreaming Gender Issues

The mainstreaming of gender issues was highlighted from the R2BD+ initiative. The following are some key areas where gender equality was emphasized:

- **Male Dominated Decision Making Structures:** Traditional social norms often prioritize men in decision-making processes, which can lead to the exclusion of women being involved in R2BD+ policies.
- **Cultural Gender Roles:** Cultural perceptions of gender roles often restrict women's participation in community activities and limit their access to resources.
- **Lack of Access to Education and Information:** Barriers to education, especially for women, may hinder their ability to participate effectively in R2BD+ activities. Limited access to information can also prevent them from being involved in decision-making processes.

## Gender Equality Efforts

Efforts were made to address gender equality issues through various initiatives:

- **Gender Training:** Training programs were conducted to enhance the capacity of local governments and communities to address gender-related issues.
- **Gender Budgeting:** Allocation of resources was prioritized to support gender equality efforts in R2BD+ activities.

## Gender Mainstreaming Strategy

The strategy aimed to integrate gender equality into all aspects of R2BD+ programs. Key areas included:

- **Institutional Arrangements:** Establishing mechanisms to ensure the integration of gender equality in program planning and implementation.
- **Capacity Building:** Enhancing the capacity of local governments and communities to address gender-related issues.

## Recommendations

Recommendations were made to strengthen gender mainstreaming efforts, including:

- **Recognizing Women's Roles:** The design of benefits should consider women's roles in resource management and their contributions to household income.
- **Affirmative Action:** Affirmative action policies should be implemented to ensure equal opportunities for men and women in R2BD+ programs.

Although R2BD+ National Gender Strategy had not been implemented at this time, the definition of the gender mainstreaming plan was still in a self-stage. Some key points regarding how gender equality can be integrated into R2BD+ activities were highlighted.

- Gender issues in REDD+: sustainable engagement processes

The REDD+ Readiness process in PNG took place between 2011-2017, including in the design of the National REDD+ Strategy and other key elements to enable effective implementation. The process was designed to be inclusive and participatory, with a focus on gender equality issues and the need for women's participation.

One of the most beneficial aspects of these programs was the funding and condition of important stakeholder engagement and consultations as well as capacity of multiple stakeholders in REDD+. This included the development of gender-sensitive guidelines and conditions that were developed through extensive consultation processes. The process also included the participation of women as an active part of the planning, implementation, and monitoring of the program.

During the readiness phase, some of the staff affiliated with awareness raising on gender issues and the importance of gender equality in REDD+. This was done to ensure that the capacity building initiatives were inclusive and that the needs of both men and women were considered. The process also included the development of a gender-sensitive monitoring system for the program.

Multi-stakeholder engagement around REDD+ established beyond the affiliation of the NRE and the specific GCF RBP programs which aimed to be implemented as helpful governance knowledge sharing platforms. These programs have been designed to include a range of training, meeting, and consultation best practices that are gender-sensitive. The process also included the development of a set of guidelines for the implementation of the program. Table 2 below presents an overview of these processes.

## Other key takeawable conclusions

The USAID Learning program in Africa’s Forest (LEAF) program was also instrumental in assisting with REDD+ activities between 2011 to 2016. Key partnerships were established with the Government of Ghana, the Government of Mozambique, and the Government of the United Republic of Tanzania. These programs focused on capacity building for local communities and government officials in the Adaptive Learning of Making Payments. Part of these actions included the development of a national REDD+ strategy and the establishment of a national REDD+ agency. This was essential to education on gender equity and gender engagement.

Most of the general awareness, knowledge sharing and capacity building was conducted through the USAID LEAF program in collaboration with the Government of Ghana, the Government of Mozambique, and the Government of Tanzania. The Government of Ghana has also established a national REDD+ agency and 21 local government units of a District Gender Committee.

The program also looked into REDD+ policy and legislation in PNG and the implementation of REDD+ on the ground. It sought to highlight the importance of further assisting the level of engagement of women’s groups, indigenous peoples, and local communities. This was done through gender balanced decision-making across all sectors.

# Gender in governance settings and conflict resolution

## REDD+ Gender Issues

The most extensive study carried out on gender and REDD+ is provided by Silvian Ngalale and Recommendations for Improving Gender Inclusion and Participation in Papua New Guinea's Forest Sector. The study was conducted to assess the current status of women's participation in decision-making processes at the national level, and to identify the challenges that hinder their participation. The study also highlighted the need for promoting gender equality and women's empowerment in REDD+ implementation and future actions.

The Gender Assessment highlighted that the employment of women and gender equality are important issues in the forestry sector. # Gender Action Plan

## 4.2 Gender Considerations in National REDD+ Social and Environmental Safeguards

The application of the UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards requires them to be adapted to the reality of each country's specific context and conditions, including their social and environmental safeguards. Given this, the CCD has issued a REDD+ Social and Environmental Safeguards Policy (REDD+ SESP) that provides guidance on how to ensure the proper implementation of the seven (7) UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards. We aspire to be part of the PNSI as an active contributor in this process. The PNSI has arranged a description of each safeguard's adherence to national circumstances by using the safeguard information system (SIS). This will help the government and stakeholders to monitor the safeguards' implementation, and to ensure that they are being implemented properly.

With this national interpretation of the REDD+ safeguards, the following general consideration was incorporated:

- The right to participate, in particular for customary owners, local communities and women's groups. It is recognized and promoted under the National REDD+ Safeguard Policy (REDD+ SESP) that the rights of indigenous peoples are protected.
- Gender equality and the right to free, prior and informed consent.

In addition, PNSI's first 50 points indicate on how safeguards have been addressed that:

The application of the UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards requires the full and effective participation of local communities and indigenous peoples in planning and implementing REDD+. This also includes the need to respect customary ownership and use rights. Local communities are to be effectively engaged in the development, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of safeguard policies and measures.

### Gender Safeguard 1.D

The Convention recognizes "Gender Equality" and the Right of women owners to participate in all decision-making processes related to REDD+.

Gender discrimination issues are addressed within specific aspects of the public service and gender equality access. These are discussed under public policy programs.

# S. CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

Papua New Guinea has national and international legal frameworks on gender and human rights that provide guidance for promoting the rights of women and communities. However, despite these frameworks, there is a significant gap between policy and practice. There are numerous challenges to achieving gender equality in Papua New Guinea. These include the lack of implementation of existing policies, inadequate resources allocated to gender-related matters, as well as strengthening gender coordination to ensure synergies, participation, protection of rights, and the development of gender-responsive budgets.

The Government has demonstrated its commitment to addressing these issues by implementing the design and implementation of the BEED-189 Project. A sustainable outcome from this project is a demand for a broader range of women's and gender equality activities that are integrated into national policies and programs. This includes mainstreaming gender in all sectors, including agriculture, health, education, and other areas.

- Identification of gender gaps and inequalities within PNG's land tenure system.
- Strengthening the capacity of women to participate in decision-making processes at the local level and other marginalized groups.
- PNG around thematic area of relevance to BEED-1 action such as gender equality, women’s participation, land use, tenure, environment, etc.

Women's participation has been guaranteed through the establishment coordinating mechanisms. Women's representation is ensured within all levels of decision-making processes. This includes ensuring that women are represented at a national level including male-dominated governance structures, executive gender equality bodies, and other relevant institutions. It also includes ensuring that women have access to the benefits of the land reform mechanism, policy research, and regional cooperation.

However, given the long-term nature of PNG's REED-1 strategy at the time when the study was undertaken, it is important to ensure that these strategies are implemented in a way that is consistent with the objectives of the BEED-189 Project. This includes ensuring that the project is aligned with the objectives of the BEED-189 Project and that it is consistent with the objectives of the BEED-189 Project.

As understood in the REED funding program and its accompanying ISMF which underpins the BEED-189 Project, the project aims to ensure that gender equality is achieved through a range of activities. This includes ensuring that women have access to land and other resources, as well as promoting gender equality in education and health. It also includes promoting gender equality in the workplace and in the community.

The Government has demonstrated its commitment to achieving these objectives by establishing a number of mechanisms to promote gender equality. These include the establishment of a Gender Equality Commission, the development of a National Action Plan for Women's Empowerment, and the establishment of a Gender Equality Fund. The Government has also committed to promoting gender equality in the workplace through the implementation of policies such as the Equal Employment Opportunity Act.

# ANNEX 1: THE GENDER ACTION PLAN (GAP)

PNG is committed to ensuring women, as well as other vulnerable groups, fully participate, enjoy equal rights and benefit from the benefits of REDD+ (Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and forest Degradation), including the role of traditional knowledge in addressing climate change. PNG will ensure that the gender action plan is integrated into the national REDD+ strategy and action plan, and on-the-ground activities to be financed with GCF projects, as well as other relevant international frameworks on gender equality and women's and girl's empowerment and inclusion.

PNG will also ensure that gender-related data is collected and used in a timely manner. PNG will fully implement the Gender Action Plan (GAP) ensuring compliance with such as the UN's Gender Policy and IUCN's Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) policy, and other relevant international guidelines and best practices.

Built on the findings from the GA, its recommendations, and the gender provisions in PNG's National REDD+ Strategy, the Gender Action Plan (GAP) for PNG is a section presented in the approved Gender Action Plan. The GAP will be implemented by the Department of Environment and Conservation (DEC), with support from the Social Management Framework for REDD+.

The GAP aims to progress initiatives to address and track progress on those proposed gender equality outcomes, including the following:

- Ensure that gender-related data is collected and used in a timely manner.
- Ensure that gender-related data is collected and used in a timely manner.
- Ensure that gender-related data is collected and used in a timely manner.

It is important to note that to ensure adequate compliance as stated in the Projects' ESM, the Gender Action Plan (GAP) will be updated annually. The GAP will also include the preparation of regular reports on progress towards achieving the outcomes, including regular monitoring and evaluation of gender-related data collection and use. This will help identify gaps and challenges in the implementation and enable adjustments where necessary.

The action plan aims to strengthen SEIA rat in customary land management, benefit-sharing mechanisms, and gender-responsive safeguards. It also aims to ensure that gender equality is integrated into the development and implementation of the SEIA development process, such as a gender-responsive safeguard system and gender-responsive monitoring and evaluation systems.

Furthermore, the availability of gender-related data is the key factor which commonly limits the ability to monitor progress on gender equality outcomes. The lack of gender-related data can also limit the ability to make progress in gender-related issues and to ensure that gender equality is integrated into the development and implementation of gender outcomes. This data can help track progress, identify gaps, and inform future actions.

Leading to more effective and sustainable project outcomes. This type of gender-related data will be generated through the project:

- Women-to-men ratios among target beneficiaries in various sectors and action areas (e.g., farming, forest activities, conservation initiatives), or other sustainable livelihoods.
- Training and capacity-building programs targeting women and men.
- Assessments of existing male and female capacities and gaps, focusing on key intervention areas such as sustainable land use planning/farming, conservation, sustainable forest management, etc.
- Assessments on existing male and female capabilities or gaps within the key implementing sectors (e.g., agriculture, livestock, fisheries), including gender-specific needs and opportunities for intervention (e.g., knowledge of sustainable land use planning/farming options).
- Data on the specific types of benefits received from field activities, such as farming equipment, training, etc. (including whether these are received by men and women).
- Training data on existing male and female capacities and gaps within the key implementing sectors.
- Gender disaggregation of the project, disaggregated by male and female beneficiaries.
